66. “’Tis like there may.”多半有，十之八九。 ↩︎

===Original===
67. “rally their best friends to choose.” That is, make as much fun of them as they like. ↩︎

===Chinese===
67. “rally their best friends to choose.”意即：随他们高兴，尽可拿最好的朋友打趣个够。 ↩︎

===Original===
68. “like a deputy-lieutenant’s hall.” That is, with all sorts of arms. The horns of the cuckold were often spoken of as armament. ↩︎

===Chinese===
68. “like a deputy-lieutenant’s hall.”意即：各式兵器一应俱全。乌龟头上的角，时常被当作军备谈论。 ↩︎

===Original===
69. “cap of maintenance.” A cap or hat which was a sign of high office, carried before a sovereign or person of high authority in a procession. ↩︎

===Chinese===
69. “cap of maintenance.”尊严帽（cap of maintenance），一种高官之位的标识帽冕，在仪仗行列中捧行于君主或大权在握者之前。 ↩︎

===Original===
70. “I’ll set his hand in.” See him well started. ↩︎

===Chinese===
70. “I’ll set his hand in.”意即：看着他顺顺当当地开了头。 ↩︎

===Original===
71. “how … lady?” Just what are your feelings toward your lady? ↩︎

===Chinese===
71. “how … lady?”你对你家夫人究竟作何感想？ ↩︎

===Original===
72. “Sir John Suckling.” A famous lyric and dramatic poet of the early seventeenth century. ↩︎

===Chinese===
72. “Sir John Suckling.”约翰·萨克林爵士，十七世纪初叶著名的抒情诗人兼剧作家。 ↩︎

===Original===
73. “Thyrsis, a youth … train.” A line from a poem of Edmund Waller. ↩︎

===Chinese===
73. “Thyrsis, a youth … train.”埃德蒙·沃勒（Edmund Waller）一首诗中的诗句。 ↩︎

===Original===
74. “I prithee … slight toy.” These and some of the following lines are Suckling’s. ↩︎

===Chinese===
74. “I prithee … slight toy.”这几行及以下若干行均出自萨克林之手。 ↩︎

===Original===
75. “all a case.” It is all the same. ↩︎

===Chinese===
75. “all a case.”全是一回事。 ↩︎

===Original===
76. “Like Phoebus … boy.” A further line from the same poem by Waller. ↩︎

===Chinese===
76. “Like Phoebus … boy.”沃勒同一首诗中的又一行。 ↩︎

===Original===
77. “in things of common application.” In the affairs of everyday life. ↩︎

===Chinese===
77. “in things of common application.”在日常生活的种种事务里。 ↩︎

===Original===
78. “douceurs, ye sommeils du matin.” Sweetnesses, ye morning naps. ↩︎

===Chinese===
78. “douceurs, ye sommeils du matin.”法语，意为「甜甜蜜蜜，尔等清晨的酣眠」。 ↩︎

===Original===
79. “hogs’ bones, hares’ gall … cat.” A playful exaggeration of some of the popular nostrums of the day. ↩︎

===Chinese===
79. “hogs’ bones, hares’ gall … cat.”对当时若干流行秘方的一种打趣的夸大。 ↩︎

===Original===
80. “Barbados waters.” A cordial flavoured with orange-peel. ↩︎

===Chinese===
80. “Barbados waters.”一种以橙皮调香的甜酒。 ↩︎

===Original===
81. “an unsized camlet.” Camlet was a light stuff of wool and linen, formerly from the East. Unsized, that is, unstiffened, not sized. ↩︎

===Chinese===
81. “an unsized camlet.”羽纱是一种毛麻混纺的轻薄料子，旧时来自东方。unsized 意即未上浆、不加浆挺。 ↩︎

===Original===
82. “*noli prosequi.*” To be unwilling to prosecute. An acknowledgment by the plaintiff that he will not press a suit further. ↩︎

===Chinese===
82. “*noli prosequi.*”（不愿再行控告。）原告以此承认不再将讼案推进下去。 ↩︎

===Original===
83. “my dear Lacedemonian.” Applied to Petulant on account of his power as an “epitomizer of words” as Witwoud says. ↩︎

===Chinese===
83. “my dear Lacedemonian.”以此称呼佩特兰特，是因为——照威特伍德的说法——他素有「字句节缩师」的本事（拉栖代梦人素以言辞简省著称）。 ↩︎

===Original===
84. “and Baldwin yonder.” The name of the fox in the beast-epic *Reynard the Fox*, also applied to the ass by Chaucer. ↩︎

===Chinese===
84. “and Baldwin yonder.”鲍德温是兽形史诗《列那狐的故事》里那只狐狸的名字；乔叟也曾用它称呼驴子。 ↩︎

===Original===
85. “A Gemini … you.” Gemini, the name for the twin stars Castor and Pollux was often used of pairs of things. ↩︎

===Chinese===
85. “A Gemini … you.”双子星卡斯托与波吕克斯之名，常用来指成对的东西。 ↩︎

===Original===
86. “Borachio.” A villain, follower of Don John, in Shakespeare’s *Much Ado About Nothing*. Borachio is the Spanish term for a leather wine bottle, hence used for a drunkard. ↩︎

===Chinese===
86. “Borachio.”波拉奇奥，莎士比亚《无事生非》中唐·约翰的党羽、一个恶棍。borrachio 在西班牙语里指皮酒囊，故引申指酒鬼。 ↩︎

===Original===
87. “bastinadoed with broomsticks.” That is, beaten on the soles of the feet. ↩︎

===Chinese===
87. “bastinadoed with broomsticks.”即用笞杖打脚心。 ↩︎

===Original===
88. “Salopian.” An inhabitant of Salop or Shropshire. ↩︎

===Chinese===
88. “Salopian.”即萨洛普（什罗普郡）人。 ↩︎

===Original===
89. “a ballad-monger.” A seller of ballads. In eighteenth-century London these were sold upon the streets by itinerant pedlars. ↩︎

===Chinese===
89. “a ballad-monger.”卖歌谣的小贩。十八世纪的伦敦，此类歌谣由流动小贩沿街叫卖。 ↩︎

===Original===
90. “Frisoneer gorget.” A piece of apparel for the neck, a kerchief, made of Frisoneer, perhaps the same word as Frison or frieze, a woollen stuff originally made in Friesland. The word Frisoneer does not apparently occur elsewhere. ↩︎

===Chinese===
90. “Frisoneer gorget.”一种颈间的穿戴，即披巾，以 Frisoneer 料子制成；此词或许就是 Frison 或 frieze（起绒粗呢），原产弗里西亚的毛织物。Frisoneer 一词似乎不见于他处。 ↩︎

===Original===
91. “a cast servingman.” A servingman that has been discharged. ↩︎

===Chinese===
91. “a cast servingman.”被辞退了的仆役。 ↩︎

===Original===
92. “and been put upon his clergy.” Forced to plead the benefit of the clergy, or privilege of exemption from capital punishment because of an ability to read and write. ↩︎

===Chinese===
92. “and been put upon his clergy.”被迫援引「教士恩典」——即因识文断字而免受死刑的特权。 ↩︎

===Original===
93. “meddle or make.” Have anything to do with the affair. ↩︎

===Chinese===
93. “meddle or make.”插手其事。 ↩︎

===Original===
94. “Abigails and Andrews.” Abigail was a common name for a lady’s maid; Andrew for a valet. ↩︎

===Chinese===
94. “Abigails and Andrews.”阿比盖尔是贴身侍女的通称，安德鲁是贴身男仆的通称。 ↩︎

===Original===
95. “Philander.” A lover in Ariosto’s *Orlando Furioso* ruined by the lustful Gabrina. Here merely a lover with an uncomplimentary allusion to Foible. ↩︎

===Chinese===
95. “Philander.”菲兰德，阿里奥斯托《疯狂的罗兰》中的一个情人，被淫荡的加布里娜毁掉。此处仅指情人，兼带对福伊布尔的一层不敬暗讽。 ↩︎

===Original===
96. “I’ll Duke’s-place you.” Marry you in a hurry as they do at Duke’s-place, Aldgate, where St. James’s Church was situated, a place notorious for irregular marriages. ↩︎

===Chinese===
96. “I’ll Duke’s-place you.”像人们在阿尔德门杜克广场——圣詹姆斯教堂所在、以不合规婚姻臭名远扬的地方——办事那样，火速把你嫁掉。 ↩︎

===Original===
97. “a Bridewell-bride.” A loose woman committed to a prison for vagrants and social criminals. The prison was supposed to stand over the well of St. Bride. ↩︎

===Chinese===
97. “a Bridewell-bride.”被投入流浪者与风化罪犯监狱的荡妇。那监狱据说正建在圣布莱德井上。 ↩︎

===Original===
98. “a brass counter.” A small piece of metal used as a token and in accounting. ↩︎

===Chinese===
98. “a brass counter.”一种小铜片，用作代币筹码，也用于记账。 ↩︎

===Original===
99. “in a quoif like a man-midwife.” The legal costume of the day included a hood. ↩︎

===Chinese===
99. “in a quoif like a man-midwife.”当日的法律袍服里本就包括一顶紧帽。 ↩︎

===Original===
100. “Doomsday Book.” A survey of England taken in 1086. ↩︎

===Chinese===
100. “Doomsday Book.”《末日审判书》，1086 年对英格兰所作的清丈。 ↩︎

===Original===
101. “cantharides.” A medicament used for blistering. ↩︎

===Chinese===
101. “cantharides.”斑蝥，一种发泡药。 ↩︎

===Original===
102. “the Temple.” One of the Inns of Court, where students at law were educated. ↩︎

===Chinese===
102. “the Temple.”「圣殿」，律师学院之一，法律学生在此受业。 ↩︎

===Original===
103. “exceeding the barbarity of a Muscovite husband.” The Russian was often used in the eighteenth century as the symbol of roughness and cruelty. ↩︎

===Chinese===
103. “exceeding the barbarity of a Muscovite husband.”十八世纪常拿俄国人充当粗野与残虐的象征。 ↩︎

===Original===
104. “from his Czarish majesty’s retinue.” An allusion to the visit of the Czar, Peter the First, three years before. ↩︎

===Chinese===
104. “from his Czarish majesty’s retinue.”暗指三年前沙皇彼得一世的驾临。 ↩︎

===Original===
105. “while the instrument is drawing.” While the agreement is being drawn up. ↩︎

===Chinese===
105. “while the instrument is drawing.”指文契正在起草之际。 ↩︎

===Original===
106. “By’r Lady.” By Our Lady. ↩︎

===Chinese===
106. “By’r Lady.”即 “By Our Lady”（凭我主圣母）。 ↩︎

===Original===
107. “o’ the quorum.” Certain justices of the peace whose presence was essential to constitute a bench. ↩︎

===Chinese===
107. “o’ the quorum.”指若干特任治安法官，惟其到场方能组成合议法庭。 ↩︎

===Original===
108. “an old fox.” A colloquial name for a sword. ↩︎

===Chinese===
108. “an old fox.”剑的俗称。 ↩︎

===Original===
109. “a mittimus.” A command in writing to a jailer to keep the person in custody in close confinement; here the vellum upon which such an order might be written. ↩︎

===Chinese===
109. “a mittimus.”收押令：谕狱卒将人犯严密监禁的书面命令；此处指可用来誊写此种命令的羊皮纸。 ↩︎

===Original===
110. “bear-garden flourish.” A flourish suitable for a bear-garden. Bear-baiting formed one of the lowest types of amusement in seventeenth-century London. These places were the scenes of many brawls. ↩︎

===Chinese===
110. “bear-garden flourish.”配得上熊园的架势。纵犬噬熊是十七世纪伦敦最低等的娱乐之一；这类场所素来是斗殴的渊薮。 ↩︎

===Original===
111. “Messalina’s poems.” Messalina was the wife of the emperor Claudian. Her name is constantly associated with incontinence. ↩︎

===Chinese===
111. “Messalina’s poems.”梅萨利娜是皇帝克劳狄乌斯的皇后，其名恒与淫佚相连。 ↩︎

===Original===
112. “paid in kind.” In order to realize the full sense of this play upon words one must bear in mind that the idea of children was seldom separated from the word kind. ↩︎

===Chinese===
112. “paid in kind.”要领会这一双关的全部意味，须记着：kind 一词极少与「子嗣」的观念分开（in kind 既指「以实物偿付」，kind 又兼「种、嗣」之义）。 ↩︎

===Original===
> *Audire est operae pretium, procedere recte*
> *Qui maechis non vultis*.
> — Horace1

===Chinese===
> 尔等于奸夫淫妇素无好感，且听他们如何四面受阻——此一听闻，值得尔等费神。
> ——贺拉斯（Horace）（1）

===Original===
> *Metuat doti deprensa*.
> — Horace2

===Chinese===
> 女子若被拿住，须为嫁妆担惊。
> ——贺拉斯（2）

## Epilogue / 收场白

===Original===
After our Epilogue this crowd dismisses,  
I’m thinking how this play’ll be pulled to pieces.  
But pray consider, e’er you doom its fall,  
How hard a thing ’twould be to please you all.  
There are some critics so with spleen diseased,  
They scarcely come inclining to be pleased:  
And sure he must have more than mortal skill  
Who pleases anyone against his will.  
Then, all bad poets we are sure are foes,  
And how their number’s swelled the town well knows  
In shoals, I’ve marked ’em judging in the pit;  
Though they’re on no pretence for judgment fit,  
But that they have been damned for want of wit.  
Since when, they, by their own offences taught,  
Set up for spies on plays, and finding fault.

===Chinese===
念罢收场白、遣散这一场看客之后，  
我已在盘算这出戏将如何被撕成碎片。  
但请诸位先想一想，再判它倾覆，  
要讨好你们所有人，谈何容易。  
有些批评家害着忧郁症的痼疾，  
进场时几乎就不存满意的念头：  
而违着本人的意愿仍能讨其欢心，  
这般人想必身怀远超凡人的本领。  
再者，坏诗人笃定个个是仇敌，  
他们的人丁如何兴旺，满城尽知——  
成群结队，我见他们在池座里执掌评断；  
尽管他们全无半点资格妄下判断，  
只除了一条：自己曾因才思不济挨过倒彩。  
打那以后，他们被自家的过失调教明白了，  
便挂牌充当戏剧的暗探，专事挑剔。

===Original===
Others there are whose malice we’d prevent:  
Such, who watch plays, with scurrilous intent  
To mark out who by characters are meant:  
And though no perfect likeness they can trace,  
Yet each pretends to know the copied face.  
These, with false glosses, feed their own ill-nature,  
And turn to libel what was meant a satire.  
May such malicious fops this fortune find,  
To think themselves alone the fools designed:  
If any are so arrogantly vain,  
To think they singly can support a scene,  
And furnish fool enough to entertain.  
For well the learned and the judicious know,  
That satire scorns to stoop so meanly low,  
As any one abstracted fop to show.  
For, as when painters form a matchless face,  
They from each fair one catch some different grace,  
And shining features in one portrait blend,  
To which no single beauty must pretend:  
So poets oft do in one piece expose  
Whole *belles assemblées* of coquettes and beaux.

===Chinese===
另有一些人，其恶意我们想先行拦阻：  
这等人看戏，怀着腌臜恶毒的用心，  
要指认剧中的角色写的是何人：  
虽然他们临摹不出一张完全相符的脸，  
却个个自称认得那被摹绘的原貌。  
这等人便拿曲解之词，饲养自家的坏心肠，  
把本意的讽刺，硬扭成诽谤。  
愿这些恶毒的花花公子落得这般报应：  
自以为戏中设计的傻瓜，唯独指向他们：  
倘若有谁傲慢虚荣到这般地步，  
竟以为单凭一人便撑得起一幕戏，  
供给满场取乐所需的全部蠢材——  
因为博学而具眼力的人深知，  
讽刺之神不屑俯就到这般卑下，  
以致单单去描画某一个孤零零的纨绔。  
因为，正如画家要合出一张绝世的面孔，  
便从每位美人身上撷取一别样的风韵，  
把明艳的五官融汇于一幅肖像，  
任何单一的美人都不容僭越：  
诗人也每每在一部作品里铺陈  
整整几厅卖弄风情的名媛与纨绔子弟。

## Prologue / 开场诗

===Original===
Of those few fools, who with ill stars are curst,  
Sure scribbling fools, called poets, fare the worst:  
For they’re a sort of fools which Fortune makes,  
And, after she has made ’em fools, forsakes.  
With Nature’s oafs ’tis quite a diff’rent case,  
For Fortune favours all her idiot race.  
In her own nest the cuckoo eggs we find,  
O’er which she broods to hatch the changeling kind:7  
No portion for her own she has to spare,  
So much she dotes on her adopted care.

===Chinese===
生逢恶星、命数不祥的傻瓜本就寥寥，  
而涂鸦的傻瓜——所谓诗人——下场最糟：  
因为这一种傻瓜是命运亲手造出，  
造罢，又被她抛下不顾。  
天生的呆子则全然另当别论，  
命运对她那一族白痴个个宠护。  
我们在她自家巢里发现杜鹃的蛋，  
她伏上去，孵那偷换来的一族（7）：  
亲生儿女的嫁资她一无所出，  
她对收养的骨肉竟溺爱到这般地步。

===Original===
Poets are bubbles, by the town drawn in,  
Suffered at first some trifling stakes to win:  
But what unequal hazards do they run!  
Each time they write they venture all they’ve won:  
The squire that’s buttered still, is sure to be undone.8  
This author, heretofore, has found your favour,  
But pleads no merit from his past behaviour.  
To build on that might prove a vain presumption,  
Should grants to poets made admit resumption,  
And in Parnassus he must lose his seat,  
If that be found a forfeited estate.

===Chinese===
诗人是水泡，被这座城吸了进去，  
起初还由着他们赢些不值一提的小注：  
可他们冒的险是何等悬殊！  
每回动笔，都把前番赢来的悉数押上：  
那位黄油长涂不辍的乡绅，管保输得精光（8）。  
这位作家，往昔也曾蒙诸位垂青，  
却不敢援引旧日行止，自居其功。  
在那上头筑基，恐属虚妄的自专，  
倘若赐与诗人的恩典竟许追夺，  
他便须从帕纳索斯山（Parnassus）上失去座席，  
倘若那被查明是一份可没收的产业。

===Original===
He owns, with toil he wrought the following scenes,  
But if they’re naught ne’er spare him for his pains:  
Damn him the more; have no commiseration  
For dullness on mature deliberation.  
He swears he’ll not resent one hissed-off scene,  
Nor, like those peevish wits, his play maintain,  
Who, to assert their sense, your taste arraign.  
Some plot we think he has, and some new thought;  
Some humour too, no farce—but that’s a fault.  
Satire, he thinks, you ought not to expect;  
For so reformed a town who dares correct?  
To please, this time, has been his sole pretence,  
He’ll not instruct, lest it should give offence.  
Should he by chance a knave or fool expose,  
That hurts none here, sure here are none of those.  
In short, our play shall (with your leave to show it)  
Give you one instance of a passive poet,  
Who to your judgments yields all resignation:  
So save or damn, after your own discretion.

===Chinese===
他自认，以下诸幕是辛勤琢就，  
但若一无是处，莫因辛劳而姑息：  
只管加倍地贬他；不必有半点恻隐——  
对那深思熟虑之后仍属的平庸。  
他立誓：哪怕一幕被嘘下台，也绝不含恨，  
也决不像那些乖戾的才子，死命袒护己作，  
为伸张自家见识，竟提审诸位的趣味。  
我想他有些布局，也有些新思；  
还有些诙谐，并无闹剧——不过这已是桩过错。  
讽刺一道，他想，诸位不该指望；  
这般风气已革新的城，谁敢来针砭？  
此番他唯一的标榜，只在取悦，  
他不愿垂教，唯恐有所冒犯。  
倘他万一揭了一个恶棍或蠢材，  
在座无人受伤——座中谅必并无此等人物。  
总之，我们这出戏（蒙俯允一演）  
要给诸位看一位俯首帖耳的诗人的实例，  
他向诸位的裁断全然俯首：  
或褒或贬，悉凭诸位卓裁。
